2
]
achieved this goal in their setting by describing the energy jumps through a cost function
c_{t}
, which is defined by minimizing the energy-dissipation integrals.
In this section, we prove an analogous result for certain transition rules
\bar{\omega}
by characterizing the energy jumps through
actions
, which play a similar role as the cost function
c
in
[
2
, Equation (2.4)]
, but whose value depends on the specific transition rule
\bar{\omega}
used in the construction.

4.1.
Generalizing from the gradient flow

To investigate transition rules which admit a characterization of the energy jumps, we start by considering transition rules which bring our framework closest to the one in
[
2
]
, i.e., transition rules arising from the gradient flow, which fulfill the following equation:

(4.1)

\bar{\omega}_{t}(x)=\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\gamma(s),

where
\gamma
is a gradient flow trajectory of
E_{t}
starting at
x
, i.e.,
\gamma(0)=x

and, for all
s>0
,

(4.2)

E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(\gamma(s))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\gamma(\tau))+|\dot{\gamma}(\tau)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

For such transition rules arising from the gradient flow, we have, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
t\in(0,T)
,
t_{1}^{n}\uparrow t
and
t_{2}^{n}\downarrow t
,

\displaystyle E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(\bar{\omega}_{t}(x))

\displaystyle=c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x,\bar{\omega}_{t}(x))\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and hence (by
Equation

3.3
)

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mu^{\delta}(\{t\})

\displaystyle=c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\big(\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta}(t_{1}^{n}),\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta}(t_{2}^{n})\big),

where
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is defined as follows:

(4.4)

c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(u_{1},u_{2}):=\inf\left\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\left(|\partial E_{t}|^{2}\big(\gamma(s)\big)+|\dot{\gamma}(s)|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\;\middle|\;\begin{subarray}{c}a<b\in\mathbb{R}\\
\gamma\in AC([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\\
\gamma(a)=u_{1},\gamma(b)=u_{2}\end{subarray}\right\}.

Let us assume for a moment that we can pass
Equation

4.3
to the limit as
\delta\rightarrow 0
,
i.e., that we can prove that, for all
\delta_{n}\rightarrow 0
,

t_{1}^{n}\rightarrow t
,
t_{2}^{n}\rightarrow t
,

(4.5)

\displaystyle E_{t}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{n}_{1})\right)-E_{t}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{n}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{n}_{1}),\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{n}_{2})\right),

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}(\{t\})

\displaystyle=c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\left(\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{n}_{1}),\lim_{n\to\infty}\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{n}_{2})\right),

where we recall that
\mu^{\delta_{n}}\rightharpoonup^{*}\bar{\mu}
as in
Equation

3.16
.
In the case (
4.5
) held, we would immediatly gain two results:

(1)

Since
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})
is nonzero
whenever
x
and
x^{\prime}
belong to different path-connected components
of critical points of
E_{t}
,

Equation

4.5
would immediatly imply that
3
holds.
This would, in turn, guarantee the existence of a limit curve
\hat{u}
through
Theorem

3.9
.

(2)

Through the energy balance (
3.17
) and
Equation

4.5
,
we would obtain the equality

\hat{E}\left(\hat{\eta}^{-}(t)\right)-\hat{E}\left(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t)\right)=c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\left({\eta}^{-}(t),{\eta}^{+}(t)\right),

where
\hat{\eta}^{\pm}(t)=[(t,\eta^{\pm}(t))]
.
Informally, this implies that the limit curve
\hat{u}
does not jump through energy barriers.

Some other interesting transition rules—e.g., those which can be derived from certain discretizations
of
Equation

4.2
—allow for equalities similar to
Equations

4.3
and
4.4
as well, with some modifications.
In this section, we develop a framework to deal with those transition rules in a unified way,
and to pass the equalities of the form of
Equation

4.3
to the limit as
\delta\rightarrow 0
,
as in
Equation

4.5
.
On our way to do so, we first describe a sufficient condition to be able to pass to the limit for
\delta\rightarrow 0
,
which has the form
of a continuous-in-time triangular inequality of the action
c_{t}
in
3’
.
Afterwards, we see that this continuous-in-time triangular inequality is fulfilled by actions tailored around
Equation

4.4
.

Definition 2
.

An action
(c_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, henceforth simply written as
c_{t}
, is a family of lower semicontinuous functions
{c_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)}
,
parametrized by
t\in[0,T]
, such that for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,T]
:

(4.6)

E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(x^{\prime})\leq c_{t}(x,x^{\prime}).

A transition rule
\bar{\omega}_{t}
is
compatible with an action
c_{t}
,
if for every
x\in\mathbb{X}
and every
t\in[0,T]
:

E_{t}(x)-E_{t}\big(\bar{\omega}_{t}(x)\big)=c_{t}\big(x,\bar{\omega}_{t}(x)\big).

Axiom 3’
.

Let the family of mappings
\bar{\omega}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
indexed by
t\in[0,T]
be the transition rule as in
Definition

1
.
Then, we require that
\bar{\omega}_{t}
is compatible with an action
c_{t}
, which has
the following property: For all
\varepsilon,C>0,t\in[0,T],K\subseteq\mathbb{X}
compact,
there exists
\Delta>0
such that:

(4.7)

\displaystyle\forall x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in K,\,\forall t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in[t-\Delta,t+\Delta]:\ c_{t}(x_{1},x_{n})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\varepsilon

whenever

(4.8)

\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\leq C.

Furthermore, for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\{y\in\mathbb{X}\mid|\partial E_{t}|(y)=0\}
,
we have that
c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=0
if and only if
x
and
x^{\prime}
belong to the same path-connected component of
\mathscr{C}
.

The following lemmas hold.

Lemma 4.1
.

3’
implies
1
.

Proof.

As the compatibility of
\bar{\omega}_{t}
with the action gives
E_{t}(x)-E_{t}\big(\bar{\omega}_{t}(x)\big)=c_{t}\big(x,\bar{\omega}_{t}(x)\big)
for every
x\in\mathbb{X}
and for every
t\in[0,T]
(cf.
Definition

2
),
1
follows from the nonnegativity of
c_{t}
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
.
Given a non-negative decreasing sequence
(\delta_{n})_{n}
such that
\delta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, let
\eta^{\delta_{n}}\colon[0,T]\to\mathbb{X}
be the discrete quasistatic evolutions constructed according to Definition
1
and starting from
x_{0}\in\mathbb{X}
.
Let us further assume that along
(\eta^{\delta_{n}})_{n}
the convergences reported in (
3.16
) hold and that
2
,
1
and
3’
are satisfied.
For every
t\in[0,T]
, let us consider
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
and two sequences
(t_{1}^{n})_{n},(t_{2}^{n})_{n}\subset[0,T]
such that
t_{1}^{n}\leq t_{2}^{n}
for every
n
,
t_{1}^{n},t_{2}^{n}\to t
as
n\to\infty
, and such that
\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{1}^{n})\to x_{1}
and
\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{2}^{n})\to x_{2}
as
n\to\infty
.
Then,

(4.9)

\bar{\mu}(\{t\})\geq c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}),\qquad E_{t}(x_{1})-E_{t}(x_{2})\geq c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}),

i.e.,
3
holds.

Before we prove
Lemma

4.2
, let us remark how it characterizes the energy jumps of the limit curve
\hat{\eta}
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
and that
1
,
2
and
3’
are satisfied.
Then, the limit trajectory
\hat{\eta}
constructed according to
Theorem

3.9

fulfills, for all
t\in[0,T]
:

\hat{E}\big(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t)\big)-\hat{E}\big(\hat{\eta}^{-}(t)\big)=\bar{\mu}(\{t\})=\hat{c}_{t}\big(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t),\hat{\eta}^{-}(t)\big),

where
\hat{c}_{t}\big([(t,x)],[(t,x^{\prime})]\big)\coloneqq c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The conclusion of
Lemma

4.2
is a strengthening of
3
.
For the last part of the corollary,
note that
3’
implies in particular that
c_{t}

fulfills the triangular inequality for a fixed
t
. Together
with the last part of
3’
, this ensures that
\hat{c}_{t}
factors through
\mathbb{X}
as described by
\hat{c}_{t}
.
Combining
Equation

4.6
with

Theorem

3.9
, we get that

\hat{E}\big(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t)\big)-\hat{E}\big(\hat{\eta}^{-}(t)\big)=\bar{\mu}(\{t\})\leq\hat{c}_{t}\big(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t),\hat{\eta}^{-}(t)\big).

Further combining this inequality with the reverse inequality in
Equation

4.9
finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

Let us fix
t\in[0,T]
and
\varepsilon>0
. From
Lemma

3.3
we have that there exists a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{X}
such that
{\rm Im}(\eta^{\delta_{n}})\subset K
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, by
Lemma

3.1
and since
E
and
\partial_{t}E
are bounded in
K
, we can choose
C
such that
\mu^{\delta_{n}}((0,T])\leq C
for all
n
. In particular, we notice that for every
n

\sum_{t^{\prime}_{i}\in J^{\delta_{n}}}c_{t_{i}^{\prime}}\left(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{\prime}_{i}),\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{\prime}_{i+1})\right)\leq\mu^{\delta_{n}}((0,T])\leq C\,.

Let
\Delta>0
be as in
3’
.For any
t_{1},t_{2}
such that

t-\Delta\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq t+\Delta
we thus have that

\displaystyle c_{t}\left(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{1}),\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}^{\prime}}\left(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{\prime}_{i}),\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t^{\prime}_{i+1})\right)+\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\mu^{\delta_{n}}((t_{1},t_{2}])+\varepsilon,

where
t^{\prime}_{i}\in J^{\delta_{n}}
are the jump points of
\eta^{\delta_{n}}
in the interval
(t_{1},t_{2}]
.
Hence, for all
0<\Delta^{\prime}\leq\Delta
it holds

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}([t-\Delta^{\prime},t+\Delta^{\prime}])

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu^{\delta_{n}}([t-\Delta^{\prime},t+\Delta^{\prime}])\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu^{\delta_{n}}((t_{1}^{n},t_{2}^{n}])

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}c_{t}\left(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{1}^{n}),\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{2}^{n})\right)-\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})-\varepsilon,

where we used the lower semicontinuity of
c_{t}
in the last line.
If we let
\Delta^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
, we see that
\bar{\mu}(\{t\})\geq c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})-\varepsilon
.
Taking the limit as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we get the first inequality in (
4.9
).
For the second one, we observe that

\begin{split}E_{t}(x_{1})-E_{t}(x_{2})&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(E_{t_{1}^{n}}\big(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{1}^{n})\big)-E_{t_{2}^{n}}\big(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t_{2}^{n})\big)\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\mathcal{E}_{t_{1}^{n}}(\eta^{\delta_{n}})-\mathcal{E}_{t_{2}^{n}}(\eta^{\delta_{n}})\right)\\
&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\int_{t_{1}^{n}}^{t_{2}^{n}}\mathcal{D}^{\delta_{n}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\mu^{\delta_{n}}((t_{1}^{n},t_{2}^{n}])\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu^{\delta_{n}}((t_{1}^{n},t_{2}^{n}]),\end{split}

and we conclude by using the same arguments as before.
∎

To construct actions fulfilling
3’
,
we will now investigate a possible proof strategy to show that the transition rule arising from
the gradient flow, as in
Equations

4.1
,
4.2
,
4.4
and
4.3
,
fulfills
3’
.
Discussing the proof strategy will allow us to distill sufficient properties
for more general transition rules for which
3’
holds true.

The resemblance of
Equation

4.7
in
3’
to the triangular inequality
already suggests a strategy to show that the transition rule (
4.1
) corresponding to the gradient flow fulfills
3’
:
Similar to the usual approach for proving the triangular inequality, we could attempt to
concatenate near-optimal curves from
x_{i}
to
x_{i+1}
to construct a competitor curve from

x_{1}
to
x_{n}
.
At first glance, this naïve proof strategy appears promising. Suppose that near-optimal curves

\gamma_{i}\colon[a_{i},b_{i}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
are chosen such that
b_{i}=a_{i+1}
and denote their
concatenation by
\bar{\gamma}
. From
Equation

4.4
, we have

\displaystyle c_{t}(x_{1},x_{n})\leq

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\int_{a_{1}}^{b_{n-1}}\left(|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\bar{\gamma}(s))+|\dot{\bar{\gamma}}(s)|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}\left(|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\gamma_{i}(s))+|\dot{\gamma}_{i}(s)|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}\left(|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}(\gamma_{i}(s))+|\dot{\gamma}_{i}(s)|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hfill+\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{2}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}(|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\gamma_{i}(s))-|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}(\gamma_{i}(s)))\,\mathrm{d}s}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\,\left(c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\varepsilon_{i}\right)+\varepsilon^{\prime}.

Here,
\varepsilon_{i}
can be controlled by selecting near-optimal curves
\gamma_{i}
, and

\varepsilon^{\prime}
can be managed by choosing
\Delta
sufficiently small. However, it is impossible
to select a
\Delta
that makes
\varepsilon^{\prime}

uniformly
small because we cannot control
the time spans
b_{i}-a_{i}
of the curves
\gamma_{i}
. Moreover,
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is expected to grow with
n
,
yet we require a
\Delta
that is effective for all
n
.
To be able to control
\varepsilon^{\prime}
, we need to modify
\bar{\gamma}

such that it does not spend too much time in areas where

|\partial E_{t}|^{2}
is significantly larger than
|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}
.
This way, we hope to ensure that
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is bounded by a constant.

To modify
\bar{\gamma}
to enable such a bound, our initial idea is to partition
K^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{X}
—where
K^{\prime}
is some compact such that
\im(\bar{\gamma})\subseteq K^{\prime}
—into two
distinct regions:

Expensive Region
: Areas where
\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|(x)
—and, for sufficiently
small
\Delta
, also
\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}(x)
—exceeds a certain threshold

P_{\mathrm{exp}}
.

Cheap Region
: Areas where
\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)
is below a threshold

P_{\mathrm{cheap}}
.

We leave
\bar{\gamma}
unchanged in the expensive region,
following along the curves
\gamma_{i}
. Whenever
\bar{\gamma}
enters the cheap region,
we take a shortcut to the point where
\bar{\gamma}
exits the cheap region (see
Figure

3
).
The total time spent in the
expensive region by
\bar{\gamma}
is bounded, which consequently bounds
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
This is because, if we call
S_{\text{exp}}^{i}\subseteq[a_{i},b_{i}]
the time spent in the expensive region
by
\gamma_{i}
, we have, using the bound
C
assumed in
3’
in
Equation

4.8
:

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{i}S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{i}\int_{S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i}}P_{\mathrm{exp}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{P_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{i}\int_{S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i}}\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}(\gamma_{i}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{P_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{i}\frac{1}{2}\int_{a_{i}}^{b_{i}}\left(|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}(\gamma_{i}(s))+|\dot{\gamma}_{i}(s)|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{P_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{i}\big(c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\varepsilon_{i}\big)\leq\frac{C+\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}{P_{\mathrm{exp}}}.

Since we can control
\varepsilon_{i}
by choosing near-optimal curves
\gamma_{i}
, we can find a bound of the total time spent
in the expensive region—independendly of
n
.
Thus, we can make
\varepsilon^{\prime}
arbitrarily small by choosing
\Delta
sufficiently small.
However, controlling the cost of shortcuts through the cheap region remains challenging.

To address this, we introduce a strategy of how to choose the two regions: We enlarge the set of critical
points in
K
—where
|\partial E_{t}|=0
—by a radius
r
, forming a cheap region
\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}

where
\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)\leq P_{\text{cheap}}\coloneqq\max_{x\in\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}}\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)
. Traversing through sets of critical
points incurs no cost, which means that the cost of each shortcut is
determined by the part of the shortcut which does
not
go through of critical points.
By taking the shortest route to and from the next critical point at the beginning and the end
of each shortcut, respectively, we can control the cost of each shortcut by
r
and
P_{\text{cheap}}
.
Furthermore, we can make both
P_{\mathrm{cheap}}
and
r
small by choosing
r
: Due to the continuity of

|\partial E_{t}|
,
P_{\text{cheap}}\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow 0
.

What remains to be bounded is the
number
of shortcuts.
Because the set of critical points can be expressed as the disjoint union of well-seperated compacts by
5
,

K
contains a finite number of connected components of critical points.
Enlarging each of these components by
r
does not increase the number of connected components,
so
\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
contains a finite number of connected components as well.
Additionally, by consistently taking a shortcut to the last point where
\bar{\gamma}
exits a connected component of
\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
, we ensure that each
connected component of
\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
is traversed at most once — limiting the number of shortcuts.

Finally, leveraging the continuity of
|\partial E_{t}|
,
\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)
is
bounded below in
K^{\prime}\setminus\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
, which makes
K^{\prime}\setminus\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}
an expensive region and enables the application of our initial strategy.

X_{1}
X_{2}
X_{3}
X_{4}
x_{in}^{1}
\overline{x}_{in}^{1}
x_{out}^{1}
\overline{x}_{out}^{1}
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma_{3}
K_{1}\colon\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)=0
K_{r}\colon\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)\leq P_{cheap}
K^{\prime}\backslash K_{r}\colon\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t_{i}}|^{2}(x)\geq P_{exp}
candidate curve

Figure 3
.

The proof strategy for
Lemma

4.8
and the scheme
for shortening competitor curves: We create a shortcut from the first point where a curve enters a
neighborhood
B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{i})
to the last point where one exits
B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{i})
, where
\mathscr{C}_{i}
is a connected component of critial points. This approach ensures
that
B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{i})
is traversed at most once. In the formal proof, rather than constructing
a new competitor curve by concatination, we employ the triangular inequality to estimate

c_{t}(X_{1},X_{4})\leq\mathfrak{c}_{t}(X_{1},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{1})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{in}}^{1},\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{1})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{1},\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{1})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{out}}^{1},X_{4})
.

From this discussion, we are now in a position to distill the essential properties of the gradient flow
that allow for a proof of
3’
.
As those properties are satisfied by a range of transition rules —whether they are discrete or additionally incorporate momentum—, the application of the resulting framework is not limited to the gradient flow.
To allow for a unified exposition of such actions, we consider curves in the phase space.
As a guide to the formal proof,
Figure

3
should be
understood as a schematic representation of this phase space.

In the following, we denote
(a,b]\cap\mathbb{I}
and
[a,b]\cap\mathbb{I}
as
(a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}
and
[a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}
, respectively, whenever

\mathbb{I}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}\}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{I}
.

Definition 3
.

An action
c_{t}
is
generated by curves
if there exists an index set

{\mathbb{I}\in\{\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{R}\}}
, a
phase space

\mathscr{P}
equipped with a metric
d_{\mathscr{P}}
and functions

\begin{array}[]{rlrlrlrl}\Gamma:&&\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}&&&\rightarrow&&2^{\{\gamma:[a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}\mid a<b\in\mathbb{I}\}}\\
&&(p_{1},p_{2})&&&\mapsto&&\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2}),\\
i:&&\mathbb{X}&&&\rightarrow&&\mathscr{P},\\[3.00003pt]
\mathcal{L}:&&\mathscr{P}\times[0,T]&&&\rightarrow&&[0,+\infty)\\
&&(p,t)&&&\mapsto&&\mathcal{L}_{t}(p),\\
\end{array}

such that for every
\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
it holds
\gamma(a)=p_{1}
and
\gamma(b)=p_{2}
,
i
is a closed immersion,
\mathcal{L}
is continuous, and
c_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
is given as

(4.10)

\begin{split}&c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathfrak{c}_{t}(i(x_{1}),i(x_{2}))\qquad\text{for $x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}$},\\
&\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2}):=\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})}\int_{(a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma_{s})\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)\qquad\text{for $p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathscr{P}$},\\
\end{split}

where
\nu
is the Lebesgue measure if
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{R}
and the counting measure if
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{Z}
.
We call the value of
\Gamma
the
set of admissible curves
for
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathscr{P}
, and the value of
\mathcal{L}
the
price

for
p\in\mathscr{P}
and
t\in[0,T]
.

Moreover, we require that the following properties hold:

Property 1:

Restrictions, concatenations and shifts of admissible curves are again admissible curves.
In other words, for
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathscr{P}
and
a\leq s\leq b\in\mathbb{I}
, the following holds:

•

Restrictions:
For all
\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{3})
such that
\dom(\gamma)=[a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}
and
\gamma(s)=p_{2}
, we have that
\gamma|_{[a,s]_{\mathbb{I}}}\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
and
\gamma|_{[s,b]_{\mathbb{I}}}\in\Gamma(p_{2},p_{3})
.

•

Concatenations:
For all
\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma(p_{2},p_{3})
such that
\dom(\gamma)=[a,s]_{\mathbb{I}}
and
\dom(\gamma^{\prime})=[s,b]_{\mathbb{I}}
, we have that
\gamma\cup\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{3})
,
where
\gamma\cup\gamma^{\prime}:[a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is defined as

\gamma\cup\gamma^{\prime}(s)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\gamma(s)&\text{if }s\in[a,s]_{\mathbb{I}},\\
\gamma^{\prime}(s)&\text{if }s\in(s,b]_{\mathbb{I}}.\end{cases}

•

Shifts:
For all
\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
such that
\dom(\gamma)=[a,b]
and all
r\in\mathbb{I}
, we have that
\gamma^{\prime}\colon[a-r,b-r]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
with
\gamma^{\prime}(t)=\gamma(t+r)
is in
\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
.

Property 2:

For
p\in\mathscr{P}
,
\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)=0
if and only if there exists
x\in\mathbb{X}
such that
i(x)=p
and

|\partial E_{t}|(x)=0
.

Property 3:

For
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
with
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
,
c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})=0
if and only if
i(x_{1})
and
i(x_{2})

lie in the same connected component of
\{p\in\mathscr{P}\mid\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)=0\}
.

Property 4:

For all
K\subseteq\mathscr{P}
compact, there exists some
K^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
compact
such that for all
p_{1},p_{2}\in K
and all
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
\gamma\colon[a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
such that
\im(\gamma)\subseteq K^{\prime}
and

\int_{(a,b]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\big(\gamma(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)\leq\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})+\varepsilon.

This means that for points
p_{1},p_{2}\in K
, we may take the infimum in
Equation

4.10

over only those admissible curves whose image lies in
K^{\prime}
.

Property 5:

For all
K\subseteq\mathscr{P}
compact and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists some
L,P>0
such that for all
p_{1},p_{2}\in K

such that
\mathcal{L}_{t}(p_{1})\leq P
,
\mathcal{L}_{t}(p_{2})\leq P
, and
d_{\mathscr{P}}(p_{1},p_{2})\leq L
, we have that
\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})\leq\varepsilon
.
Informally, this means that the value of the action is small between two points which are close to each other and where
\mathcal{L}_{t}
is small.

Remark 17
.

Notice that in what follows, the expressions of the functions appearing in
Definition

3
will be energy-dependent.

4.2.
Examples of transition rules

Before we show how the abstract
Definition

3
relates to
3’
,
we present some examples of actions—and the corresponding transition rules—that are generated by curves.
The fact that those actions indeed satisfy the required properties is contained in
Propositions

4.4
,
4.5
and
4.6
, whose proofs are postponed to
Section

5
.

4.2.1.
The gradient flow

Definition 4
.

A
transition rule of the gradient flow
is a transition rule
\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
for which there
exists, for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
, a curve
\phi\in AC([0,\infty),\mathbb{X})
and a sequence
(s_{k})_{k\geq 1}
with
s_{k}\nearrow+\infty
as
k\to\infty
such that

\phi(0)=x
,
\lim_{k\to\infty}d\left(\phi(s_{k}),\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x)\right)=0
, and, for all
s\in[0,\infty)
,

E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(\phi(s))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}\big(\phi(\sigma)\big)+|\dot{\phi}(\sigma)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.

The
action of the gradient flow
is the action
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
given by

(4.11)

c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\inf\left\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\left(|\partial E_{t}|^{2}\big(\phi(s)\big)+|\dot{\phi}(s)|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\;\middle|\;\begin{subarray}{c}a<b\in\mathbb{R}\\
\phi\in AC([a,b],\mathbb{X})\\
\phi(a)=x_{1},\phi(b)=x_{2}\end{subarray}\right\}.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let us assume
4
,
1
,
2
,
7
,
5
and
7
. A transition rule
\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
of the gradient flow is
compatible—as defined in
Definition

2
—with the action
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
of the gradient flow.
Furthermore, the action
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is generated by curves as per
Definition

3
,
where
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{R}
and where we use the following definitions:

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\text{, where }d_{\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}}\big((x_{1},v_{1}),(x_{2},v_{2})\big)=d(x_{1},x_{2})+|v_{1}-v_{2}|

(4.13)

\displaystyle i^{{}^{{}_{F}}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(x,0)

(4.14)

\displaystyle\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\gamma\colon[a,b]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}\,\middle|\,\begin{subarray}{c}a<b\in\mathbb{R},\gamma(a)=p_{1},\,\gamma(b)=p_{2},\\
\gamma_{x}\in AC([a,b];\mathbb{X}),\,|\dot{\gamma}_{x}|\equiv\gamma_{v}\text{a.e.}\end{subarray}\right\}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\big((x,v)\big)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(x)+\frac{1}{2}v^{2}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}^{F}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})}\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

Here, for every interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we denote the components of a curve
\gamma\colon I\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}
by
\gamma_{x}\colon I\to\mathbb{X}
and
\gamma_{v}\colon I\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

4.2.2.
The minimizing movement scheme

Definition 5
.

For
\tau>0
,
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,T]
, a
minimizing movement scheme sequence starting at point
x
at time
t

is a sequence
(u_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{X}

such that
u_{0}=x
, and,
for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

u_{s+1}\in\argmin_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\left\{E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(y,u_{s})^{2}\right\}.

A
transition rule of the minimizing movement scheme
is a transition rule
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}

for which there exists, for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,T]
, a minimizing movement scheme sequence
(u_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{X}
starting at point
x
at time
t

such that
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(x)
is a cluster point of
(u_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
.
The
action of the minimizing movement scheme
is the function
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
given by

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\inf\left\{\sum_{s=a}^{b}E_{t}(u_{s})-E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(u_{s})+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(u_{s+1},u_{s})^{2}\;\middle|\;\begin{subarray}{c}a\leq b\in\mathbb{Z},\\
(u_{s})_{s\in[a,b]_{\mathbb{Z}}},\\
u_{a}=x_{1},u_{b}=x_{2}\end{subarray}\right\},

where we set
u_{b+1}=u_{b}=x_{2}
and

(4.18)

E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x):=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\left\{E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(x,y)^{2}\right\}.

We mention that in
[
25
, Definition 5.14]
the authors introduced an action for the minimizing movement scheme in Euclidean setting which is fully compatible with
Equations

4.17
and
4.18
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
4
,
3
,
5
and
7
. For
\tau>0
, a transition rule
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}
of the minimizing movement scheme is compatible—as defined in
Definition

2
—with the action
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}
of the minimizing movement scheme.

Moreover, if
\tau\leq\frac{1}{L}
,
the map
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
is an action generated by curves as per
Definition

3
,
where
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{Z}
and where we use the following definitions:

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\text{, where }d_{\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}}(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\left(d(x_{0},x^{\prime}_{0})+d(x_{1},x^{\prime}_{1})\right)

(4.20)

\displaystyle i^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(x,x)

(4.21)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\gamma\colon[a,b]_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}\,\middle|\,\begin{subarray}{c}a<b\in\mathbb{Z},\,\gamma(a)=\underline{x},\gamma(b)=\underline{x}^{\prime},\\
\gamma_{1}(s)=\gamma_{0}(s+1)\text{ for }s\in[a,b-1]_{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}\right\}

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(\underline{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq E_{t}(x_{0})-E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x_{0})+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(x_{0},x_{1})^{2},

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathfrak{c}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})}\,\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(\gamma(s)),

where
E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
is defined as in
Equation

4.18
. Here, for every
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
we denote the components of a curve
\gamma\colon I\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
by
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, and we denote the components of points
\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}=\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
by
(x_{0},x_{1})\coloneqq\underline{x}
and
(x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1})\coloneqq\underline{x}^{\prime}
.

4.2.3.
The BDF2 method for the gradient flow

Definition 6
.

For
\tau>0
,
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,T]
, a
BDF2 discretization sequence starting at point
x
at time
t
is a sequence
(u_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{X}

such that
u_{0}=x
and,
for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

u_{s+1}\in\argmin_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\left\{E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{\tau}d(y,u_{s})^{2}-\frac{1}{4\tau}d(y,u_{s-1})^{2}\right\},

where we set
u_{-1}=x
. A
transition rule of the BDF2 discretization
is a transition rule
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
for which there exists, for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
t\in[0,T]
, a BDF2 discretization sequence
(u_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{X}
starting at point
x
at time
t

such that
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}(x)
is a cluster point of
(u_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
.
The
action of the BDF2 discretization
is the map
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
given by

(4.24)

\begin{split}\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\coloneqq\inf\left\{\sum_{s=a}^{b+1}\right.\bigg(&E_{t}(u_{s})-E^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t,\tau}(u_{s},u_{s-1})+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(u_{s+1},u_{s})^{2}\\
&+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(u_{s},u_{s-1})^{2}-\frac{1}{4\tau}d(u_{s+1},u_{s-1})^{2}\bigg)\left.\;\middle|\;\begin{subarray}{c}a\leq b\in\mathbb{Z},\\
(u_{s})_{s\in[a,b]_{\mathbb{Z}}},\\
u_{a}=x_{1},u_{b}=x_{2}\end{subarray}\right\},\end{split}

where where we set
u_{a-1}=x_{1}
,
u_{b+1}=u_{b+2}=x_{2}
and

(4.25)

E^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t,\tau}(x,x^{\prime}):=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\left\{E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{\tau}d(y,x)^{2}-\frac{1}{4\tau}d(y,x^{\prime})^{2}\right\}.

To the best of our knowledge, the action for the BDF2 scheme provided by
Equations

4.24
and
4.25
is completely original.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let us sassume
1
,
2
,
4
,
3
,
5
and
7
. For
\tau>0
, a transition rule
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}
of the BDF2 discretization is compatible—as defined in
Definition

2
—with the action
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}
of the BDF2 discretization.

Moreover, for every
\tau\leq\frac{1}{L}
,
the map
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
is an action generated by curves as per
Definition

3
,
where
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{Z}
and where we use the following definitions:

(4.26)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\text{, where }d_{\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}}(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i=-1}^{1}d(x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})

(4.27)

\displaystyle i^{B}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(x,x,x)

(4.28)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\gamma\colon[a,b]_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}\,\middle|\,\begin{subarray}{c}a<b\in\mathbb{Z},\,\gamma(a)=\underline{x},\gamma(b)=\underline{x}^{\prime},\\
\gamma_{1}(s-1)=\gamma_{0}(s)=\gamma_{-1}(s+1)\text{ for }s\in[a+1,b-1]_{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}\right\}

(4.29)

\displaystyle\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}(\underline{x})

\displaystyle:=E_{t}(x_{0})-E^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t,\tau}(x_{0},x_{-1})+\frac{1}{2\tau}\left(d(x_{0},x_{1})^{2}+d(x_{-1},x_{0})^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{4\tau}d(x_{-1},x_{1})^{2},

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathfrak{c}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})}\,\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t}(\gamma(s)),

where
E^{{}^{{}_{B}}}_{t,\tau}
is defined as in
Equation

4.25
. Here, for every
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, we denote the components of a curve
\gamma\colon I\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}
by
\gamma_{-1}
,
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, and we denote the components of points
\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{B}}}=\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
by
(x_{-1},x_{0},x_{1})\coloneqq\underline{x}
and
(x^{\prime}_{-1},x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1})\coloneqq\underline{x}^{\prime}
.

4.3.
A rigorous proof of
3’

With those examples in mind, we now show that actions generated by curves fulfill
3’
.
We postpone the proof of the following ancillary lemma to the end of this section.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
c_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
be an action generated by curves as per
Definition

3
and let
\mathfrak{c}_{t}\colon\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}\to[0,\infty)
be the map defined in (
4.10
).
Then,
\mathfrak{c}_{t}
and
c_{t}
fulfill the triangular inequality,
i.e., for every
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathscr{P}
, every
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{X}
, and every
t\in[0,T]
we have

\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{3})\leq\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{2},p_{3})\qquad\text{and}\qquad c_{t}(x_{1},x_{3})\leq c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+c_{t}(x_{2},x_{3})\,.

We are now ready to prove the key lemma of this section.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
. If a transition rule
\bar{\omega}_{t}
is compatible with an action
c_{t}
generated by curves, then
3’
holds.

Proof.

We follow the idea outlined on pages
4.1
–
4.1

and illustrated in
Figure

3
.
In the first part of the proof, we obtain a
\Delta>0
as demanded by
3’
.
In the second part, we confirm that with such a choice of
\Delta>0
and for any
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
,

t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
as in
3’
,
Equation

4.7
is indeed fulfilled.

Let
\varepsilon,C>0
,
t\in[0,T]
and
K\subseteq\mathbb{X}
compact be given as in
3’
.
We first notice that
i(K)
is compact, as
i
is continuous. Hence, we may find a compact set
K^{\prime}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
related to
i(K)
as prescribed by
4
.
By
2
and
5
, since
i
is a closed immersion, the connected components of
\{p\in K^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)=0\}
are well-separated,
and hence there are only finitely many of them.
We enumerate those connected components as
\mathscr{C}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{C}_{m}
and use

5
to choose
L,P_{\mathrm{cheap}}>0
such that for
all
p_{1},p_{2}\in K^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{L}_{t}(p_{1})\leq P_{\mathrm{cheap}}
,
\mathcal{L}_{t}(p_{2})\leq P_{\mathrm{cheap}}
and
d_{\mathscr{P}}(p_{1},p_{2})\leq L
, we have that

(4.31)

\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6(m+1)}.

For
r>0
, we write
\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{j})
, where
B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{j})\coloneqq\{p\in\mathscr{P}\mid d_{\mathscr{P}}(p,\mathscr{C}_{j})<r\}
.
By the continuity of
\mathcal{L}
and the compactness of
K^{\prime}
,

\sup_{p\in\overline{(\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r})}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)\rightarrow 0
as
r\rightarrow 0
.
Thus, we can choose
0<r<L
such that
\sup_{p\in\overline{(\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r})}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)\leq P_{\mathrm{cheap}}
. Moreover, by further reducing
r
if needed, we can assume that
\overline{B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{j})}\cap\overline{B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{j^{\prime}})}=\emptyset
whenever
j\neq j^{\prime}
.
Using a similar argument, we see that

\inf_{p\in K^{\prime}\setminus\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)>0.

Even more, we can choose some
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that

(4.32)

\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in K^{\prime}\setminus\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r}\\
t^{\prime}\in[t-\Delta^{\prime\prime},t+\Delta^{\prime\prime}]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{t^{\prime}}(p)>0.

Using continuity one last time, we can choose some
\Delta^{\prime}>0
such that for all
p\in K^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}\in[t-\Delta^{\prime},t+\Delta^{\prime}]
,
we have that

(4.33)

|\mathcal{L}_{t^{\prime}}(p)-\mathcal{L}_{t}(p)|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathcal{L}}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\cdot\left(\frac{C+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}{\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}}+2(m+1)\right)^{-1}.

We claim that
\Delta\coloneqq\min\{\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime}\}
is the
\Delta>0
desired by
3’
.

To prove this, we fix points
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in K
and instants
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in[t-\Delta,t+\Delta]

such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\leq C
.
We first choose
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma\big(i(x_{i}),i(x_{i+1})\big)
according to
4

such that
\im(\gamma_{i})\subseteq K^{\prime}
and

(4.34)

\int_{(a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)\leq c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\frac{\varepsilon}{3n},

where
a_{i}<b_{i}\in\mathbb{I}
are such that
\dom(\gamma_{i})=[a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}
.
We set
IS:=\{(i,s)\mid i\in\{1,\dots,n\},s\in[a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}\}
and

\displaystyle IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}:=\left\{(i,s)\in IS\;\middle|\;\gamma_{i}(s)\in\overline{(\underline{\mathscr{C}}_{r})}\right\}.

Note that for all
i
,
\{s\in\mathbb{I}\mid(i,s)\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}\}
is closed
and that for all
(i,s)\in IS\backslash IS_{c}
, we have by
Equation

4.32
that

(4.35)

\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))\geq\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}.

For the ease of notation, we use the lexicographic order on the pairs
(i,s)\in IS
,
where

(i,s)<(j,s^{\prime})
if
i<j
or if
i=j
and
s<s^{\prime}
.
For
(i,s)\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}
, we also set
J(i,s)
to be the unique index
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that
\gamma_{i}(s)\in B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{j})
.

As a next step, we inductively define breakpoints, which we will eventually use to get an upper bound
on
c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})
by repeatedly using the triangular inequality.
We start by setting

(4.36)

(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{0},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{0}):=\begin{cases}\max\{(i,s)\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}\mid J(i,s)=J(1,a_{1})\}&\text{if }(1,a_{1})\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}},\\
(1,a_{1})&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and inductively set, for each
k

\displaystyle(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{(i,s)\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}\mid(i,s)>(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\},

\displaystyle(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k+1},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k+1})

\displaystyle:=\max\{(i,s)\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}\mid J(i,s)=J(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})\}.

We terminate this scheme at step
d
if there is no possible choice for
(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{d+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{d+1})
.
Formally, we set
(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{d+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{d+1})=(n,b_{n})
and
(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{0},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{0})=(1,a_{1})
.
The resulting breakpoints have the following properties:

(1)

d\leq m
(where
m
is the number of components
\mathscr{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{C}_{m}
).

(2)

(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}
and
(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}

for all
1\leq k\leq d
.

(3)

J(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})=J(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})
for all
1\leq k\leq d
.

(4)

For all
(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})<(i,s)<(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})
, we have that

(i,s)\notin IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}
.

We set, for
0\leq k\leq d+1
,
p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k}:=\gamma_{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k}}(s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})
and
p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}:=\gamma_{i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}(s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})
.
In the rest of this proof, we first estimate
\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})
, then
\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})
,
and then we combine those estimates using the triangular inequality to get an upper bound on
c_{t}(x_{1},x_{n})
.

Estimating
\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})
:

We set, for
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
0\leq k\leq d
,

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}

\displaystyle:=\{s\in(a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}\mid(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})<(i,s)<(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})\}

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}

\displaystyle:=\{s\in(a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}\mid(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\leq(i,s)\leq(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})\},

where some of the
S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}
and
\bar{S}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}
might be empty.
By item
4
above and
Equation

4.35
,
we see that for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
0\leq k\leq d
and
s\in S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}
, we have that

\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))\geq\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle C+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\int_{(a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)\geq\sum_{k=0}^{d}\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\int_{S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}}\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k=0}^{d}\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\int_{S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}}\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)=\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{d}\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\nu(S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}).

From the chain of inequalities above and the fact that for a fixed
0\leq k\leq d
, we have that
\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\nu(\overline{S}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k})-\nu(S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k})\leq 2
,we get

(4.37)

\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\left(\sum_{k=0}^{d}\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\nu(\overline{S}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k})\right)&\leq\varepsilon_{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\left(\sum_{k=0}^{d}\left(\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\nu(S_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k})+2\right)\right)\\
&\leq\varepsilon_{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\left(\frac{C+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}}{\Delta_{\mathcal{L}}}+2(m+1)\right)=\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.\end{split}

With this estimate, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{d}\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})\leq\sum_{k=0}^{d}\left(\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\int_{\overline{S}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma_{i}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\underbrace{\int_{(a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)}_{\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3n}+c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\text{ by~\eqref{eq:choice_of_curve}}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{d}\left(\sum_{i=i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}}^{i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1}}\int_{\overline{S}_{\mathrm{exp}}^{i,k}}\underbrace{|\mathcal{L}_{t_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(s))-\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma_{i}(s))|}_{\leq\varepsilon_{\mathcal{L}}\text{ by~\eqref{eq:delta_prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)\right)}_{\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\text{ by~\eqref{eq:cost_estimate}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\frac{2\varepsilon}{3}.

Estimating
\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})
:

We fix
0\leq k\leq d
. If
k=0
,
we only consider the case where
p_{\mathrm{in}}^{0}\neq p_{\mathrm{out}}^{0}
, and we are thus in the
first case of
Equation

4.36
, i.e.,
i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k}\in IS_{\mathrm{cheap}}
.
If
p_{\mathrm{in}}^{0}=p_{\mathrm{out}}^{0}
, we may simply ignore this term in the triangular inequality at the end.
We set
j:=J(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})=J(i_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},s_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})
.
We can choose
\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}_{j}
such that

d_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})\leq r\leq L\quad\text{and}\quad d_{\mathscr{P}}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\leq r\leq L,

so that we can use
Equation

4.31
to see that

\mathfrak{c}_{t}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6(m+1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{c}_{t}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{6(m+1)}.

Using the triangular inequality and
3
, we have that

\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\leq\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{k})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(\overline{p}_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3(m+1)}.

Conclusion:

Since
(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{d+1},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{d+1})=(n,b_{n})
and
(i_{\mathrm{in}}^{0},s_{\mathrm{in}}^{0})=(1,a_{1})
, we have that

\displaystyle c_{t}(x_{1},x_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{d}\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k})+\sum_{k=0}^{d}\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{\mathrm{out}}^{k},p_{\mathrm{in}}^{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq(m+1)\frac{\varepsilon}{3(m+1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\frac{2\varepsilon}{3}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_{t_{i}}(x_{i},x_{i+1})+\varepsilon.

The fact that for
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\{y\in\mathbb{X}\mid|\partial_{t}E_{t}|(y)=0\}
,
c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=0
if and only if
x
and
x^{\prime}
belong to the same path-connected components of
\mathscr{C}

follows from
3
together with
2
.
∎

To end this section, we proof the ancillary lemma
Lemma

4.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.7
.

Let
c_{t}
be an action generated by curves as per
Definition

3
.
We fix
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathscr{P}
and
\varepsilon>0
.
We choose
\gamma^{(i)}\in\Gamma(p_{i},p_{i+1})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
such that

(4.38)

\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{i},p_{i+1})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\geq\int_{(a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\big(\gamma^{(i)}(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s),

where
a_{i}<b_{i}\in\mathbb{I}
are such that
\dom(\gamma^{(i)})=[a_{i},b_{i}]_{\mathbb{I}}
.
In particular,
\gamma^{(i)}(a_{i})=x_{i}
,
\gamma^{(i)}(b_{i})=x_{i+1}
and
\gamma^{(1)}(b_{1})=\gamma^{(2)}(a_{2})
.
We set
\underline{\gamma}^{(2)}
to be the curve
\gamma^{(2)}
reparametrized to
[b_{1},b_{2}+b_{1}-a_{2}]_{\mathbb{I}}
, i.e,

\underline{\gamma}^{(2)}(s)=\gamma^{(2)}(s+b_{1}-a_{2})
. By
1
,

\underline{\gamma}^{(2)}\in\Gamma(p_{2},p_{3})
and likewise,
the concatenation
\gamma^{(1)}\cup\underline{\gamma}^{(2)}
as defined in
1
is in
\Gamma(p_{1},p_{3})
.
We have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{3})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(a_{1},b_{2}+b_{1}-a_{2}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\big(\gamma^{(1)}\cup\underline{\gamma}^{(2)}(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(a_{1},b_{1}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\big(\gamma^{(1)}(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)+\int_{(a_{2},b_{2}]_{\mathbb{I}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\big(\gamma^{(2)}(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(s)

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})+\mathfrak{c}_{t}(p_{2},p_{3})+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, we have that
\mathfrak{c}_{t}
fulfills the triangular inequality. The triangle inequality for
c_{t}
follows by definition of
c_{t}
in (
4.10
).
∎

We are now ready to state the main result of this paper, recalling the definition of
\mathcal{X}
and
q\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{X}\mapsto\mathcal{X}
from
Equation

2.3
.

Theorem 1 (complete)
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
.
Furthermore, let the family of mappings
\bar{\omega}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
indexed by
t\in[0,T]
be the transition rule as in
Definition

1
, corresponding to an action
c_{t}
and
complying with
1
,
2
and
3
.
Then, for any positive vanishing sequence
(\delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and for the corresponding discrete quasistatic evolutions

\eta^{\delta_{n}}
constructed according to
Definition

1
we can—without relabeling—extract a subsequence
such that:

(1)

There exists a positive Radon measure
\bar{\mu}\in\mathcal{M}([0,T])
and
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathbb{R})
such that

•

\mu^{\delta^{n}}\rightharpoonup^{*}\bar{\mu}
, where
\mu^{\delta_{n}}
is as defined in
Equation

3.3
, and

•

\mathcal{D}^{\delta_{n}}\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathcal{D}\quad\mbox{in }L^{\infty}([0,T],\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\delta_{n}}(t)\coloneqq\partial_{t}E_{t}\big(\eta^{\delta_{n}}(t)\big)
.

(2)

The compositions
q\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\eta^{\delta_{n}})
converge pointwise to a piecewise continuous limiting curve
\hat{\eta}\colon[0,T]\to\mathcal{X}
.

(3)

The left and right limits
\hat{\eta}^{-}(t)
and
\hat{\eta}^{+}(t)
of
\hat{\eta}
exist for every
t\in(0,T)
, and so do the limits
\hat{\eta}^{+}(0)
and
\hat{\eta}^{-}(T)
.

(4)

The limiting curve
\hat{\eta}
fulfills, for all
0\leq s<t\leq T
, the energy balance identiy

(4.39)

\hat{E}\big(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t)\big)-\hat{E}\big(\hat{\eta}^{-}(s)\big)=\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{D}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau-\bar{\mu}([s,t]),

(5)

For all
t\in[0,T]
,
\hat{\eta}(t)\subset\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{X}\mid|\partial E_{t}|(x)=0\}
.

(6)

The limiting curve
\hat{\eta}
is continuous in
[0,T]\setminus J
, where we define
J\coloneqq\{t\in[0,T]\mid\bar{\mu}(\{t\})>0\}
.

If we assume
6’
in addition to
3
,
1
,
2
,
4
and
5
—here,
1
,
2
and
3
are sufficient—, we have that

(7)

For almost all
t\in[0,T]
, for all
(t,x)\in\hat{\eta}(t)
, we have that

\partial_{t}E_{t}(x)=\mathcal{D}(t)
. Picking any lifts
h_{t}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,T],h_{\mathbb{X}}\colon\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{X}
such that
q\circ(h_{t}\times h_{\mathbb{X}})\equiv\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{X}}
, we can thus rewrite the energy balance (
4.39
) as

(4.40)

\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}^{-}(s))-\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t))=\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{t}E_{\tau}\Big(h_{\mathbb{X}}\big(\hat{\eta}(\tau)\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}\tau-\bar{\mu}([s,t]).

If the transition rule fulfills the stronger
3’
in place of
3
,—which is the case whenever we assume in addition that the transition rule is compatible with an action generated by curves—we have, furthermore,

(8)

For every
t\in J
,

\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}^{-}(t))-\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t))=\bar{\mu}(\{t\})=\hat{c}_{t}(\hat{\eta}^{-}(t),\hat{\eta}^{+}(t)),

where
\hat{c}_{t}([(t,x_{1}],[(t,x_{2})])\coloneqq c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})
.

If we assume
6
and
7
in addition to
3
,
1
,
2
,
4
,
5
and
8
—here,
1
,
2
and
3
are sufficient and
6’
is not needed—, we even have that
\partial_{t}E_{\cdot}\colon[0,T]\times\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}
factors through
\mathcal{X}
; we call the resulting functional
\partial_{t}\hat{E}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. In this case, we even have

(9)

The limiting curve
\hat{\eta}
fulfills, for all
0\leq s<t\leq T
, the energy balance identiy

(4.41)

\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}^{-}(s))-\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}^{+}(t))=\int_{s}^{t}\partial_{t}\hat{E}(\hat{\eta}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau-\bar{\mu}([s,t]).

Furthermore, we have that
\mathrm{supp}\,\bar{\mu}=J
, i.e.,
\bar{\mu}
is purely atomic.

Proof.

Item

1
follows from
Proposition

3.5
under
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
.

Items

2
,
3
,
4
,
6
and
5
follows from
Theorem

3.9
under
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
whenever the action fulfills
1
,
2
and
3
.

Item

8
and that
3’
implies
1
follows from
Lemma

4.1
and
Corollary

4.3
under
1
,

,
3
,

and

.
The fact that
3’
is guaranteed whenever we assume in addition that the transition rule is compatible with an action which is generated by curves is the content of

.

4.3
under

,

,

,

and

whenever the action fulfills

,

and

.

The fact that assuming

,

and

in addition to

,

,

,

and

, we can rewrite the energy balance as in

follows from

. In

.

5.
Examples of transition rules

The goal of this section is to show that the transition rules of the examples given in
Section

4.2
are indeed actions which are generated by curves
as per
Definition

3
, i.e., to prove
Propositions

4.4
,
4.5
and
4.6
.
Before we dive into the specific examples, we first show a general lemma which will help us to prove
3
for all three examples.

Lemma 5.1
.

Assume
4
and
5
. Let
c_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
be an action which fulfills the triangular inequality, i.e., for which, for
x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{X}
, we have that

c_{t}(x,x^{\prime\prime})\leq c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})+c_{t}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}).

Let us furthermore assume that there exists
\alpha>0
such that for each compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{X}
there exists a constant
C_{K}>0
for which,
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in K
with
x,x^{\prime}\in\{y\in\mathbb{X}\mid|\partial E_{t}|(y)=0\}\eqqcolon\mathscr{C}
, we have that
c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\leq C_{K}\cdot d(x,x^{\prime})^{\alpha}
.
If
x,x^{\prime}
lie in the same component of
\mathscr{C}
, and there exists some connected subset
U\subseteq\mathscr{C}
such that

x,x^{\prime}\subseteq U
and
U
has Hausdorff dimension
\dim_{H}(U)<\alpha
, then
c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=0
.

Moreover, if we have that
\frac{c_{t}(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})^{\alpha}}\rightarrow 0
as
d(y,y^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
, then the same condition holds if the set
U
to have finite
\alpha
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.

Proof.

Let us pick
x,x^{\prime}
and
U
as in the assumptions. We can assume, without loss of generality, that

U
is contained in a single component of
\mathscr{C}
, and thus, by
5
and
4
,

U\subseteq K
for some compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{X}
. We pick
C_{K}
as in the assumptions.
Next, we note that if
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
is an open cover of
U
, there
exists—possibly after reordering—a sequence of distinct
y_{1},\dots,y_{n^{\prime}}\in U

such that
n^{\prime}\leq n+1
,
\{y_{i},y_{i+1}\}\subseteq U_{i}
and
y_{1}=x
,
y_{n^{\prime}}=x^{\prime}
.
To see this, we first may assume without loss of generality that
x\in U_{1}
and
pick
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
sucht that
x^{\prime}\in U_{n^{\prime}}
.
We consider the graph
G
whose vertices are given as
N\coloneqq\{U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\}

and whose edges are given by
\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\{(U_{i},U_{j})\mid U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset\}
.
Since
U
is connected,
G
is connected and we can find a minimal path from
U_{1}
to
U_{n^{\prime}}
in
G
.
After reordering, we may assume that this path is given as
U_{1},\dots,U_{n^{\prime}}
.
Setting
y_{1}=x
,
y^{n^{\prime}}=x^{\prime}
and picking
y^{i}\in U_{i}\cap U_{i+1}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}-1\}
,
we have found the desired sequence.
By applying the triangular inequality repeatedly, we have that

\displaystyle c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}-1}c_{t}(y_{i},y_{i+1})\leq\sup_{1\leq j<n^{\prime}}\frac{c_{t}(y_{j},y_{j+1})}{d(y_{j},y_{j+1})^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}-1}d(y_{i},y_{i+1})^{\alpha}.

If either the Hausdorff dimension of
U
is smaller than
\alpha
or if
\frac{c_{t}(y,y^{\prime})}{d(y,y^{\prime})^{\alpha}}\rightarrow 0
as
d(y,y^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
and the
\alpha
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
U
is finite, we can make the right hand side arbitrarily small, proving the claim.
∎

5.1.
The gradient flow

In this section, we prove
Proposition

4.4
. We use freely
Equations

4.12
,
4.13
,
4.14
,
4.15
and
4.16
in
Proposition

4.4
.
Substituting those definitions into
Equation

4.10
and simplifying, we obtain the function
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
defined in
Equation

4.11
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The map
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
coincides with
c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
defined as

(5.1)

c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\coloneqq\inf_{\phi\in AC([0,1];\mathbb{X})}\int_{[0,1]}|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s))|\dot{\phi}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s.

Furthermore, the integral on the right-hand side is invariant to reparametrization of the curve:
For all
\phi\in AC([0,1];\mathbb{X})
and all continuously differentiable and monotone increasing
f\colon[a,b]\rightarrow[0,1]
, setting

\overline{\phi}\coloneqq\phi\circ f
we have that for every
t\in[0,T]

(5.2)

\int_{[0,1]}|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s))|\dot{\phi}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{[a,b]}|\partial E_{t}|(\overline{\phi}(s))|\dot{\overline{\phi}}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s.

As a result, we have that

(5.3)

c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\inf\left\{\int_{a}^{b}|\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi(s)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\;\middle|\;\begin{subarray}{c}a<b\in\mathbb{R}\\
\phi\in AC([a,b],\mathbb{X})\\
\phi(a)=x_{1},\,\phi(b)=x_{2},\,|\dot{\phi}|\equiv 1\end{subarray}\right\}.

Proof.

Equation

5.2
is a simple application of the chain rule.
For
Equation

5.1
, we note that
for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

(5.4)

\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2}y^{2}=xy+\frac{1}{2}(x-y)^{2}\geq xy.

From (
5.4
), it follows directly that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\geq c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}
.
For the other direction, let us assume that
c_{t}^{F}(x_{1},x_{2})>0
. We first fix some
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
and
\kappa\in(0,\frac{1}{3})
. Next, we choose some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,
b>0
, and a curve
\phi\in AC([0,b],\mathbb{X})
such that

(5.5)

\int_{[0,b]}|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s))|\dot{\phi}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\leq c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+\varepsilon^{\prime}.

By
Equation

5.2
, we can assume that
|\dot{\phi}(s)|\leq 1
for a.e.
s\in[0,b]
. Let us fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and set

r_{\varepsilon}(s)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{s}\frac{|\dot{\phi}(\sigma)|+\varepsilon}{|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(\sigma))+\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\qquad\text{for $s\in[0,b]$.}

We define
\phi_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\phi\circ r^{-1}_{\varepsilon}
and note that
D\coloneqq\dom(\phi_{\varepsilon})\subseteq[0,\frac{(M+1)b}{\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}}]
.
If we set
D^{\prime}\coloneqq\{s\in D\mid\frac{|\dot{\phi}(r_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(s))|}{|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(r_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(s)))}\leq b\varepsilon^{\kappa}\}
, we see that for all
\sigma\in D^{\prime}
,
\dot{r}_{\varepsilon}(r_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(\sigma))\leq\frac{b+1}{\varepsilon^{\kappa}}
,
and thus
|D^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon^{\kappa}
, where
|D^{\prime}|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
D^{\prime}
. On the other hand, for
s\in D\setminus D^{\prime}
, we have—setting
s^{\prime}\coloneqq r_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(s)
—that

(5.6)

\displaystyle\bigg||\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)-|\dot{\phi}_{\varepsilon}|(s)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg||\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi(s^{\prime})\big)-|\dot{\phi}|(s^{\prime})\cdot\frac{|\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi(s^{\prime})\big)+\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}}{|\dot{\phi}|(s^{\prime})+\varepsilon}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg||\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s^{\prime}))-\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{|\dot{\phi}|(s^{\prime})+\varepsilon}\right)\Big(|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s^{\prime}))+\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}\Big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon+\varepsilon\frac{|\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi(s^{\prime})\big)}{|\dot{\phi}|(s^{\prime})}\leq 2\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\kappa}}{b}.

Furthermore, we can find a bound
N
such that
N\geq\big||\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi_{\varepsilon}(s)\big)-|\dot{\phi}_{\varepsilon}|(s)\big|
for all
s\in D
, independendly of the chosen
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, as both
|\dot{\phi}|
and
|\partial E_{t}|\big(\phi(s^{\prime})\big)
are bounded.
Using
Equation

5.6
and
Equation

5.4
, we see that

\displaystyle c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\phi_{\varepsilon}(s))+|\dot{\phi}_{\varepsilon}(s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{D}|\partial E_{t}|(\phi_{\varepsilon}(s))\,|\dot{\phi}_{\varepsilon}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{D}\frac{1}{2}\big(|\partial E_{t}|(\phi_{\varepsilon}(s))-|\dot{\phi}_{\varepsilon}(s)|\big)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+\varepsilon^{\prime}+\int_{D\setminus D^{\prime}}\frac{1}{2}\bigg(2\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}+\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\kappa}}{b}\bigg)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{D^{\prime}}\frac{1}{2}N^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

(5.7)

\displaystyle\leq c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+\varepsilon^{\prime}+\frac{(M+1)b\varepsilon^{2-2\kappa}(2\varepsilon^{2\kappa}+\frac{1}{b})^{2}}{2\varepsilon^{1+\kappa}}+\frac{N^{2}\varepsilon^{\kappa}}{2}.

As
\kappa
was chosen in
(0,\frac{1}{3})
, we may pass to the limit in (
5.7
) as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to obtain the inequality
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})+\varepsilon^{\prime}
. Letting
\varepsilon^{\prime}\to 0
in a second step, we obtain the thesis.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let us assume
2
and
1
. Let us consider
a<b\in\mathbb{R}
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
, and
\phi\in AC([a,b];\mathbb{X})
such that

\phi(a)=x_{1}
,
\phi(b)=x_{2}
.
Then

(5.8)

\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\big(\big(\phi(s),\dot{\phi}(s)\big)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq|E_{t}(x_{1})-E_{t}(x_{2})|.

In particular, for any two points
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we have that

(5.9)

\mathfrak{c}^{F}_{t}\big((x_{1},v_{1}),(x_{2},v_{2})\big)\geq|E_{t}(x_{1})-E_{t}(x_{2})|.

Proof.

By
Equation

5.4
, we have that

\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\big(\big(\phi(s),\dot{\phi}(s)\big)\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\int_{a}^{b}|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s))|\dot{\phi}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s.

Hence,
Equation

5.8
follows from
|\partial E_{t}|(\phi(s))
being a strong upper gradient of
E_{t}
as per
Remark

1
(see
[
7
, Definition 1.2.1]
).
Equation

5.9
follows by taking the infimum over all curves
\phi\in\Gamma\big((x_{1},v_{1}),(x_{2},v_{2})\big)
in
Equation

5.8
.
∎

Before we show that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is an action generated by curves, we will show an intermediate result.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let us assume
7
, and let
x\in\mathbb{X}
and
P,r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|\partial E_{t}|(y)\leq P
for all
y\in B_{r}(x)
.
Then, for all
x^{\prime}\in B_{r}(x)
, we have that

c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\leq P\cdot d(x,x^{\prime}).

Proof.

We pick
\varepsilon>0
arbitrary and, using
7
, an
\varepsilon
-almost geodesic with natural parametrization
\gamma
between
x
and
x^{\prime}
—where
d(x,x^{\prime})+\varepsilon\leq r
—, i.e.,
|\dot{\gamma}|\equiv 1
,

\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(b)=x^{\prime}
, where
b=d(x,x^{\prime})+\varepsilon
.
Since
|\partial E_{t}|\big(\gamma(s)\big)\leq P
for
s\in[0,b]
,
we can follow
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}\big(x,x^{\prime})\leq P\big(d(x,x^{\prime})+\varepsilon\big)
directly by using
Lemma

5.2
. Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, the claim follows.
∎

Let us show that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is actually an action.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
and
7
. Then
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is an action according to
Definition

2
. In fact,
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is even continuous.

Proof.

To prove the continuity of
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
,
we first note that in the spirit of
Lemma

4.7
,
the triangular inequality follows from the fact that
\Gamma
is closed under
restrictions, shifts and concatenations.
Combining the triangular inequality with
Lemma

5.4
,
we see that
|c_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})-c_{t}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|\leq C_{1}d(x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})+C_{2}d(x_{2},x_{2}^{\prime})
for
(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})
close to
(x_{1},x_{2})
, where
C_{1}=|\partial E_{t}|(x_{1})+1
and
C_{2}=|\partial E_{t}|(x_{2})+1
.
Finally,
Equation

4.6
, which requires that
E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(x^{\prime})\leq c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})
, follows as a special case of
Equation

5.9
.
∎

Before we prove that the action
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is generated by curves, we first investigate under which circumstances we can apply
Lemma

5.1
to
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
7
,
4
and
5
, and let
x\mapsto|\partial E_{t}|(x)
be locally
\alpha
-Hölder continuous with
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Furthermore, assume that for each
x,x^{\prime}
which lie in the same component of
\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{X}\mid|\partial E_{t}|(x)=0\}
,
there exists some connected
U\subseteq\mathscr{C}
such that
x,x^{\prime}\in U
and
\dim_{H}(U)<1+\alpha
.
Then,
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=0
whenever
x,x^{\prime}
lie in the same connected component of
\mathscr{C}
.

Furthermore, if
|\partial E_{t}|
is only continuous, we have that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})=0
whenever
x,x^{\prime}
lie in some connected
U\subseteq\mathscr{C}
with
finite
1
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.

Proof.

We start with the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
By the virtue of
Lemma

5.1
, we only need to show that for all compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{X}
,
there exists a constant
C_{K}>0
such that for all
x,x^{\prime}\in K\cap\mathscr{C}
we have that
c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\leq C_{K}\cdot d(x,x^{\prime})^{\alpha+1}
.

Let us fix a compact
K\subset\mathbb{X}
and let
D>0
be such that the
\alpha
-Hölder inequality holds for all
y,y^{\prime}\in K_{1}\coloneqq\overline{B_{1}(K)}
:

\big||\partial E_{t}|(y)-|\partial E_{t}|(y^{\prime})\big|\leq D\cdot d(y,y^{\prime})^{\alpha}.

Let
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}\cap K
. We set
\underline{d}\coloneqq d(x,x^{\prime})
and pick
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and a curve
\phi
such that

\dom(\phi)=[0,\underline{d}+\varepsilon]
,
\phi(0)=x
,
\phi(\underline{d}+\varepsilon)=x^{\prime}
and
|\dot{\phi}|\equiv 1
.
In particular,
\phi(t)\in K_{1}
for every
t\in[0,\overline{d}+\varepsilon]
.
We furthermore set the constants
\underline{\alpha}\coloneqq 1-\alpha
and
s^{\star}\coloneqq\left(\frac{\underline{d}+\varepsilon}{\underline{\alpha}}\right)^{\underline{\alpha}}
, the function
m\colon[0,s^{\star}]\to[0,\underline{d}+\varepsilon]
defined as
m(s)\coloneqq(\underline{\alpha}\cdot s)^{\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}}
, and
\underline{\phi}\coloneqq\phi\circ m
.
Then, for every
s\in[0,s^{\star}]
we have that

|\partial E_{t}|(\underline{\phi}(s))\leq D\cdot d(x,\underline{\phi}(s))^{\alpha}\leq D\cdot m(s)^{\alpha}=D\cdot(\underline{\alpha}\cdot s)^{\frac{\alpha}{\underline{\alpha}}},

and that

|\dot{\underline{\phi}}(s)|\equiv m^{\prime}(s)=(\underline{\alpha}\cdot s)^{\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}}-1}=(\underline{\alpha}\cdot s)^{\frac{\alpha}{\underline{\alpha}}}.

We now calculate

\displaystyle c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{s^{\star}}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\underline{\phi}(s))+|\dot{\underline{\phi}}(s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\underbrace{(D^{2}+1)\cdot\underline{\alpha}^{\frac{2\alpha}{\underline{\alpha}}}}_{\eqqcolon D}\int_{0}^{s^{\star}}s^{\frac{2\alpha}{\underline{\alpha}}}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\alpha}D^{\prime}}{1+\alpha}s^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\underline{\alpha}}}\Big|_{0}^{s^{\star}}=\frac{\overline{\alpha}D^{\prime}}{1+\alpha}(s^{\star})^{\frac{1+\alpha}{\underline{\alpha}}}=\underbrace{\frac{\overline{\alpha}D^{\prime}}{1+\alpha}\cdot\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}^{1+\alpha}}}_{\eqqcolon C_{K}}(\underline{d}+\varepsilon)^{1+\alpha}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, we have that
c_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\leq C_{K}\cdot d(x,x^{\prime})^{1+\alpha}
.

For
\alpha=1
, we can do the same calculation as above, but setting
m
to be the function
s\mapsto e^{s}
instead.

To show the second part of the lemma, we again do a similar calculation as above with
m
being the identity function to see
that
c_{t}(y,y^{\prime})\to 0
as
d(y,y^{\prime})\to 0
for
y,y^{\prime}\in U
. If we apply the second part of
Lemma

5.1
with
\alpha=0
,
the thesis follows.
∎

We are now ready to prove that the action
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is generated by curves.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let us assume
4
,
1
,
2
,
7
,
5
and
7
.
Then, the action
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is generated by curves as in
Definition

3
.

Proof.

The continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
on
\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}\times[0,T]

follows immediately from the continuity of
|\partial E_{t}|
on
\mathbb{X}\times[0,T]
;
likewise, the fact that
i
is a closed immersion is immediate.
As for the properties,
1
and
2
are immediate;

5
follows from
Lemma

5.4
:
For
\varepsilon>0
and
K\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}
compact,
K_{\mathbb{X}}\coloneqq p_{\mathbb{X}}(K)
, the projection
of
K
onto
\mathbb{X}
, is compact as well.
Due to the continuity of
x\mapsto|\partial E_{t}|(x)
, we can find some
r^{\prime},P
such that for all
x\in K_{\mathbb{X}}
,
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}
for which
d(x,x^{\prime})\leq r^{\prime}
and
|\partial E_{t}|(x)\leq P
,
we have that
|\partial E_{t}|(x^{\prime})\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and we can set
L\coloneqq\min(r^{\prime},\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.
To finish, we note that
\mathfrak{c}^{F}_{t}(p_{1},p_{2})
does
not
depend on the second components of
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}
.
Let us now prove the other two properties.

3
:

To see that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})=0
whenever
x_{1}
and
x_{1}
lie in the same
connected component of the set of critical points
\mathscr{C}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{X}\mid|\partial E_{t}|(x)=0\}
, we note that
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are connected by a rectifiable curve by
5
. Since a rectifiable curve has finite 1-dimensional Hausdorff measure, we can
apply
Lemma

5.6
to see that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})=0
.
For the converse, let
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
such that they do
not
lie in the same connected component of

K^{\prime}
.
Then either there is an
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(i^{{}^{{}_{F}}}(x_{i}))>0
, or
x_{1}
and

x_{2}
lie in different connected components of
\mathscr{C}
.
In the former case, there exists an
r\leq d(x_{1},x_{2})
such that
P\coloneqq\min_{x\in B_{r}(x_{i})}|\partial E_{t}(x)|>0

and, by reparametrizing any curve
\gamma
from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
such that
|\dot{\gamma}|\equiv 1

and using (
5.2
), we see that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})=c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq rP>0
.
If
x_{1},x_{2}
lie in
different components of
\mathscr{C}
, we set
\mathscr{C}_{1}
to be the component containing
x_{1}
and use
5
to choose
r
such that

|\partial E_{t}(x)|>0
for all
x\in\overline{B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{1}
. We set
D\coloneqq\overline{B_{r}(\mathscr{C}_{1})}\setminus B_{\frac{r}{2}}(\mathscr{C}_{1})

and
P\coloneqq\min_{x\in D}|\partial E_{t}(x)|>0
. Since any curve
\gamma
connecting
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
has to pass through
D
,
we can use (
5.2
) again to reparametrize
\gamma
such that
|\dot{\gamma}|=1

and see that
c^{{}^{{}_{F^{\prime}}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq\frac{r}{2}P>0
.

4
:

Fix some
K\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}
compact. Then
K_{\mathbb{X}}\coloneqq p_{\mathbb{X}}(K)
, the projection
of
K
onto
\mathbb{X}
, is compact as well.
Since
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is continuous by
Lemma

5.5
, we can set
C\coloneqq\max_{x_{1},x_{2}\in K_{\mathbb{X}}}c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2})
.
We furthermore set
E_{max}\coloneqq\max_{x\in K_{\mathbb{X}}}E_{t}(x)
.
For each
\varepsilon>0
and
p_{1},p_{2}\in K
, we set
x_{1}\coloneqq p_{\mathbb{X}}(p_{1})
and
x_{2}\coloneqq p_{\mathbb{X}}(p_{1})

the projection onto
\mathbb{X}
of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, respectively.
With this notation, let us show that the first component of every quasi-optimal curve
\gamma\in\Gamma(p_{1},p_{2})
,
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}
such that

\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\mathfrak{c}_{t}^{F}(p_{1},p_{2})+\varepsilon

stays within
K^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq E_{t}^{-1}((-\infty,Z])\subseteq\mathbb{X}
, where
Z\coloneqq E_{\max}+\frac{1}{2}(C+\varepsilon+1)
.
To see this, assume otherwise and pick
s^{\star}\in(a,b)
such that
E_{t}(\gamma_{x}(s^{\star}))>Z
.
Using
Equation

5.8
, we would then have the following contradiction:

\displaystyle C+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\geq\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{a}^{s^{\star}}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{s^{\star}}^{b}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(\gamma(s))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle>(Z-E_{max})+(Z-E_{max})=2Z-2E_{max}=C+\varepsilon+1.

By
4
,
K^{\prime\prime}
is compact.
What is left to show is that we can choose quasi-optimal curves with bounded velocity.
To this end, note that in the proof of
Lemma

5.2
,
we chose near-optimal curves
\gamma
whose velocity fulfilled

\big||\dot{\gamma}(s)|-|\partial E_{t}|(\gamma(s))\big|\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}

for arbitrarily
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
. Thus we can set

v_{max}\coloneqq\max_{x\in K^{\prime}}|\partial E_{t}|(x)+\varepsilon^{\prime}
, for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,
and
K^{\prime}\coloneqq K^{\prime\prime}\times[0,v_{max}]\subseteq\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}
.
∎

To finish this section, we prove
Proposition

4.4
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.4
.

The fact that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is an action as in
Definition

2
is the content of
Lemma

5.5
;
the fact that
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
is generated by curves as in
Definition

3
is the content of the above
Lemma

5.7
.
What is left to prove is that
\bar{\omega}^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
as defined in
Definition

4
is compatible with
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
as in
Definition

2
,
i.e., that for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
and
\phi\in AC([0,\infty),\mathbb{X})

such that
\phi(0)=x_{1}
,
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\phi(s)=x_{2}
and, for all
s\in[0,\infty)
,

E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(\phi(s))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}|\partial E_{t}|^{2}(\phi(s))+|\dot{\phi}(s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

we have that

E_{t}(x_{1})-E_{t}(x_{2})=c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}(x_{1},x_{2}).

This follows directly from the continuity of
E_{t}
and
c^{{}^{{}_{F}}}_{t}
.
∎

5.2.
The minimizing movement scheme

In this section, we will explore the minimizing movement scheme and
prove
Proposition

4.5
. We use freely the definitions
Equations

4.19
,
4.20
,
4.21
,
4.22
and
4.23
, and in the rest of this chapter adopt the convention where we denote the components of a curve
\gamma\colon I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\to\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}=\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
by
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
, and we denote the components of points
\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
, by
(x_{0},x_{1})\coloneqq\underline{x}
and
(x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1})\coloneqq\underline{x}^{\prime}
.
Substituting
Equations

4.19
,
4.20
,
4.21
and
4.22
into
Equation

4.10
and simplifying, we obtain the expression of
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}\to[0,\infty)
written in
Equation

4.17
.
As we describe in
Remark

7
, whenever we assume that
7
holds, we also assume that
\tau\leq\frac{1}{L}
, where
L
is the Lipschitz constant mentioned in
7
.
Finally, we report that in
[
25
, Definition 5.14]
the authors introduced an action for the minimizing movement scheme in Euclidean setting which is fully compatible with
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}
.

Remark 18
.

If we assume
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, then
the function
(t,x,y)\mapsto E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(x,y)^{2}
is continuous in
(t,x,y)
.
Furthermore, only points in the relatively compact sublevel set
\{y\in\mathbb{X}\mid E_{t}(y)\leq E_{t}(x)\}
contribute to the infimum in (
4.18
).
Thus,
E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
is itself continuous in
(t,x)
, as it is locally an infimum of equicontinuous functions. Hence, also
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}
is continuous in
(t,\underline{x})\in[0,T]\times\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
.
Lastly, we note that
E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x)\leq E_{t}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
:
To see this, we use
y=x
as a competitor in
Equation

4.18
.
We clarify the relation between the conditions
E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x)=E_{t}(x)
and
|\partial E_{t}|(x)=0
for

x\in\mathbb{X}
in
Lemma

5.13
.

Let us first see how this definition relates to the minimizing movement scheme.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
. Then, for all
\tau>0
,
\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})
, we have that

(5.10)

\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\big(\gamma(s)\big)\geq E_{t}(x_{1})-E_{t}(x^{\prime}_{1}),

If
\underline{x}=i^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x)
and
\underline{x}^{\prime}=i^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x^{\prime})
for some
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}
, then

(5.11)

\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\big(\gamma(s)\big)\geq E_{t}(x)-E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x^{\prime}),

where equality is attained if and only if

(5.12)

\gamma_{0}(s+1)=\gamma_{1}(s)\in\argmin_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\left\{E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{2\tau}d(\gamma_{0}(s),y)^{2}\right\}

for all
s\in[a+1,b-1]_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
In particular, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}
we have

(5.13)

\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(x,x^{\prime})\geq E_{t}(x)-E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x^{\prime})\geq E_{t}(x)-E_{t}(x^{\prime}).

Proof.

To prove
Equation

5.10
, it suffices to show that

\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\big(\gamma(s)\big)\geq E_{t}\big(\gamma_{0}(s)\big)-E_{t}\big(\gamma_{1}(s)\big)
for all
s\in[a+1,b-1]_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
To this end, we write

(5.14)

\begin{split}&\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\big(\gamma(s)\big)-\bigg(E_{t}\big(\gamma_{0}(s))-E_{t}\big(\gamma_{1}(s)\big)\bigg)\\
=\,&E_{t}\big(\gamma_{0}(s)\big)-\inf_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\left\{E_{t}(y)+\frac{1}{2\tau}d\big(\gamma_{0}(s),y\big)^{2}\right\}\\
&+\frac{1}{2\tau}d\big(\gamma_{1}(s),\gamma_{0}(s)\big)^{2}-E_{t}\big(\gamma_{0}(s)\big)+E_{t}\big(\gamma_{1}(s)\big)\\
\geq\,&E_{t}\big(\gamma_{0}(s)\big)-E_{t}\big(\gamma_{1}(s)\big)-\frac{1}{2\tau}d\big(\gamma_{1}(s),\gamma_{0}(s)\big)^{2}\\
&+\frac{1}{2\tau}d\big(\gamma_{1}(s),\gamma_{0}(s)\big)^{2}-E_{t}\big(\gamma_{0}(s)\big)+E_{t}\big(\gamma_{1}(s)\big)=0,\end{split}

where the inequality follows by using
y=\gamma_{1}(s)
as a competitor for the infimum.

Equation

5.11
follows by applying
Equation

5.10

to
\gamma|_{[a,b-1]}
and noting that
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\big(\gamma(b)\big)=\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}\big((x^{\prime},x^{\prime})\big)=E_{t}(x^{\prime})-E_{t,\tau}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x^{\prime})
.
In the lower bound
Equation

5.11
, equality is attained if and only if
\gamma_{0}(s)
fulfills (
5.12
).
Finally,
Equation

5.13
follows by taking the infimum
over
\Gamma(i^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x),i^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x^{\prime}))
in
Equation

5.11
and using
Remark

18
for the second inequality.
∎

Before we can show that
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}c^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}
is an action according to
Definition

2
, we need to show two intermediate results. The first lemma is related to
5
, the second lemma concerns
4
of
Definition

3
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let us assume
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
.
For all
\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}
and
L,P>0

such that
d_{\mathscr{P}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}}(\underline{x},\underline{x}^{\prime})\leq L
,
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(\underline{x})\leq P
,
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(\underline{x}^{\prime})\leq P
and
\mathllap{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{${}^{\tau}$}\!\!}\mathcal{L}^{{}^{{}_{M}}}_{t}(i^{{}^{{}_{M}}}(x_{1}))\leq P

we have that
